   2^j\mathsf M\!\left(\left\lceil\frac{n}{2^j}\right\rceil\right)
  \right).
\end{equation}
In particular:
\begin{enumerate}
 \item if $\mathsf M(n)=\Theta(n^\alpha)$ with $\alpha>1$, then
 $H(n)=O(\mathsf M(n))$;
 \item if $\mathsf M(n)=O(n\log^\kappa n)$ for fixed $\kappa\ge0$, then
 \[
  H(n)=O(n\log^{\kappa+1}n)
      =O(\mathsf M(n)\log n).
 \]
\end{enumerate}
The complete gcd driver has the same asymptotic coefficient-operation bound,
because successive HGCD blocks reduce the active degree geometrically.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Unfolding \eqref{eq:hgcd-recurrence} by recursion level gives
\eqref{eq:hgcd-sum-complexity}.  If
$\mathsf M(n)=\Theta(n^\alpha)$ with $\alpha>1$, the contribution of level
$j$ is
\[
 2^j\Theta\bigl((n/2^j)^\alpha\bigr)
 =\Theta\bigl(n^\alpha2^{-j(\alpha-1)}\bigr),
\]
a convergent geometric series.  For
$\mathsf M(n)=O(n\log^\kappa n)$, every nonempty level costs
$O(n\log^\kappa n)$ and there are $O(\log n)$ levels.

By Theorem~\ref{thm:gcd-hgcd-correctness}, the sizes of successive outer
HGCD blocks are $n,n/2,n/4,\ldots$ up to rounding.  Summing the displayed
HGCD bounds over these geometrically decreasing sizes changes only the
constant in the power-growth case and preserves the stated
$O(n\log^{\kappa+1}n)$ bound in the quasi-linear case.
\end{proof}

For the multiplication algorithms already developed in this chapter,
Karatsuba and fixed-order Toom multiplication have exponent $\alpha>1$.
Thus fraction-free HGCD preserves their degree exponent.  With an
asymptotically quasi-linear multiplication algorithm, the usual extra
logarithmic HGCD factor appears.

\subsection{Coefficient growth and bit complexity}

The preceding theorem counts polynomial operations; over $\ZZ[x]$ it is not by
itself a bit-complexity theorem.  The reason is the same expression-swell
phenomenon that motivated subresultant normalization, but here it also affects
the transformation matrices.

The primitive matrix step is locally optimal among \emph{common scalar}
normalizations: after replacing $M$ by $M/\operatorname{cont}(M)$, no larger
nonunit integer can be divided simultaneously from all four entries while
remaining in $\ZZ[x]^{2\times2}$.  This can remove very large predictable
powers introduced by pseudo-division.  It does not, however, give the
canonical determinantal bounds of Theorem~\ref{thm:subresultant-coefficient-growth}
or Corollary~\ref{cor:subresultant-cofactor-size}.

To make the remaining cost explicit, let $W$ be a bound for the bit size of
all coefficients occurring at some size-$n$ HGCD node.  If
$\mathcal M(n,W)$ bounds a polynomial product at that size,
$\mathcal P(n,W)$ bounds fast pseudo-division, $\mathsf G_{\ZZ}(W)$ bounds an
integer gcd and $\mathsf D_{\ZZ}(W)$ an exact integer division, then the
nonrecursive bit cost at that node is bounded by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hgcd-conditional-bit-cost}
 O\!\left(
   \mathcal M(n,W)+\mathcal P(n,W)
   +n\bigl(\mathsf G_{\ZZ}(W)+\mathsf D_{\ZZ}(W)\bigr)
  \right).
\end{equation}
The last term is the matrix-content computation and the four coefficientwise
exact divisions by the common content.

What is missing is a polynomial a priori bound for $W$ for this particular
normalization.  The single-step estimate of
Lemma~\ref{lem:pseudodiv-coefficient-growth} shows why this matters: a
pseudo-division with degree difference $O(n)$ can turn $w$-bit coefficients
into coefficients having $O(n(w+1))$ bits.  If such estimates are simply
iterated along a pseudo-remainder chain and no cancellation or common-content
removal is credited, only an exponential-in-degree coefficient bound results.
The matrix-content divisions observed in practice remove much of this growth,
but the present theory does not identify their outputs with bounded-size
subresultant minors and therefore does not turn
\eqref{eq:hgcd-conditional-bit-cost} into a useful quasi-linear bit bound.

\begin{remark}\label{rem:hgcd-experimental-status}
The fraction-free HGCD is therefore asymptotically attractive in the
\emph{polynomial degree} model and is a valid gcd algorithm, but its current
common-content normalization is weaker than the coefficient-control theory
available for fast subresultant algorithms.  This is why the companion
implementation exposes it as a separate reference/experimental gcd rather
than selecting it as the default over $\ZZ[x]$.  Brown's subresultant PRS
remains the path with a proved determinant-size coefficient bound.
\end{remark}



\section{Resultants over $\ZZ[x]$}\label{sec:resultants}

The resultant packages the question whether two polynomials have a common
root into a single scalar.  It also forms the zeroth member of the
subresultant family studied in Section~\ref{sec:subresultant-prs}.  This makes
Brown's polynomial remainder sequence a natural fraction-free resultant
algorithm: the same sequence used for gcd computation already contains the
resultant as its final lower-subresultant scalar.

Throughout this section let
\[
 A(x)=a_mx^m+\cdots+a_0,
 \qquad
 B(x)=b_nx^n+\cdots+b_0
\]
be nonzero polynomials over a field or over $\ZZ$, with $a_m b_n\ne0$.
The classical determinant and product formulas below are standard; see, for
example, \cite{Sturmfels2002,vonZurGathenGerhard2013}.  When one input is
the zero polynomial, whose degree is undefined, we adopt the companion
implementation's convention that the resultant is zero.

\subsection{The Sylvester determinant and product formula}

\begin{definition}[Sylvester matrix and resultant]
The \emph{Sylvester matrix} $\operatorname{Syl}(A,B)$ is the
$(m+n)\times(m+n)$ coefficient matrix whose first $n$ rows are the
coefficient vectors of
\[
 x^{n-1}A,\ldots,xA,A
\]
and whose final $m$ rows are the coefficient vectors of
\[
 x^{m-1}B,\ldots,xB,B,
\]
all written in the same descending monomial basis.  The resultant is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:resultant-sylvester}
 \operatorname{Res}(A,B)=\det\operatorname{Syl}(A,B).
\end{equation}
For two nonzero constants the matrix is empty and its determinant is $1$.
\end{definition}

The determinant has a useful linear-map interpretation.  If $K$ is a field,
then, up to the harmless choice of ordering of the two domain blocks,
the Sylvester matrix represents
\[
 \Phi:K[x]_{<n}\oplus K[x]_{<m}\longrightarrow K[x]_{<m+n},
 \qquad
 (U,V)\longmapsto UA+VB.
\]
Thus the determinant vanishes precisely when the two bounded families of
shifts are linearly dependent.

\begin{theorem}[Product formula]\label{thm:resultant-product-formula}
Let $L$ be a splitting field and write
\[
 A=a_m\prod_{i=1}^{m}(x-\alpha_i),
 \qquad
 B=b_n\prod_{j=1}^{n}(x-\beta_j),
\]
with roots repeated according to multiplicity.  Then
\begin{align}
 \operatorname{Res}(A,B)
 &=a_m^n\prod_{i=1}^{m}B(\alpha_i),
 \label{eq:resultant-product-a}\\
 &=a_m^n b_n^m
   \prod_{i=1}^{m}\prod_{j=1}^{n}(\alpha_i-\beta_j),
 \label{eq:resultant-product-roots}\\
 &=(-1)^{mn}b_n^m\prod_{j=1}^{n}A(\beta_j).
 \label{eq:resultant-product-b}
\end{align}
These expressions equal the Sylvester determinant
\eqref{eq:resultant-sylvester}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Work first over the splitting field $L$.  In the quotient algebra
$L[x]/(A/a_m)$, multiplication by $B$ has determinant
\[
 \prod_{i=1}^{m}B(\alpha_i).
\]
Indeed, after extending scalars if necessary, multiplication by $x$ has the
roots $\alpha_i$ as eigenvalues, counted with multiplicity; applying $B$ to
that operator gives eigenvalues $B(\alpha_i)$.  Equivalently, this follows by
putting the companion matrix of the monic polynomial $A/a_m$ into triangular
form.

Now order the codomain basis as
\[
 x^{n-1}A,\ldots,xA,A,x^{m-1},\ldots,x,1.
\]
Relative to the descending monomial basis, its change-of-basis matrix is
block triangular with determinant $a_m^n$.  Order the first domain block in
the same way.  In this adapted basis the map $\Phi$ is block triangular: the
first diagonal block is the identity on the multiples of $A$, while the second
is multiplication by $B$ modulo $A$.  Hence
\[
 \det\operatorname{Syl}(A,B)
 =a_m^n\prod_iB(\alpha_i),
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:resultant-product-a}.  Factoring $B$ gives
\eqref{eq:resultant-product-roots}.  Interchanging the two roots in every
factor introduces $(-1)^{mn}$ and gives
\eqref{eq:resultant-product-b}.
\end{proof}

Several standard laws follow immediately.

\begin{proposition}[Basic resultant identities]\label{prop:resultant-basic}
For nonzero polynomials of degrees $m,n$ and nonzero scalars $c,d$,
\begin{align}
 \operatorname{Res}(B,A)
   &=(-1)^{mn}\operatorname{Res}(A,B),
 \label{eq:resultant-symmetry}\\
 \operatorname{Res}(cA,dB)
   &=c^n d^m\operatorname{Res}(A,B),
 \label{eq:resultant-scaling}\\
 \operatorname{Res}(A_1A_2,B)
   &=\operatorname{Res}(A_1,B)\operatorname{Res}(A_2,B),
 \label{eq:resultant-multiplicative-a}\\
 \operatorname{Res}(A,B_1B_2)
   &=\operatorname{Res}(A,B_1)\operatorname{Res}(A,B_2).
 \label{eq:resultant-multiplicative-b}
\end{align}
In particular, for a nonzero constant $c$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:resultant-constant}
 \operatorname{Res}(c,B)=c^n,
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Res}(A,c)=c^m.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Equation \eqref{eq:resultant-symmetry} follows from exchanging
$\alpha_i-\beta_j$ with $\beta_j-\alpha_i$ in
\eqref{eq:resultant-product-roots}.  Scaling either input changes only its
leading coefficient and gives \eqref{eq:resultant-scaling}.  The root
multisets of a product are the disjoint union of the root multisets of its
factors, which proves multiplicativity.  The constant formulas are the
special cases with one degree equal to zero.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Common-root criterion]\label{thm:resultant-zero-criterion}
For nonzero $A,B\in K[x]$,
\[
 \operatorname{Res}(A,B)=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \gcd(A,B)\ne1
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 A\text{ and }B\text{ have a common root in }\overline K.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{eq:resultant-product-roots}, the resultant vanishes exactly when
$\alpha_i=\beta_j$ for some pair of roots.  This is equivalent to a common
linear factor over the algebraic closure, hence to a positive-degree gcd over
$K[x]$.
\end{proof}

Over $\ZZ[x]$, the determinant formula also shows directly that the resultant
is an integer.  The subresultant B\'ezout identity from
Theorem~\ref{thm:subresultant-bezout}, specialized to $j=0$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:resultant-bezout-repeat}
 \operatorname{Res}(A,B)=UA+VB,
 \qquad U,V\in\ZZ[x],
\end{equation}
with
\[
 \deg U<n,
 \qquad
 \deg V<m.
\]
When the resultant is nonzero, this is the integral certificate used in
Corollary~\ref{cor:xgcd-resultant-ideal}.

\subsection{Euclidean and pseudo-remainder recurrences}

The product formula gives a useful recurrence under polynomial division.
Unlike the integer Euclidean algorithm, a change in degree contributes a
power of the divisor's leading coefficient.

\begin{lemma}[Resultant under ordinary remainder]\label{lem:resultant-remainder}
Let $K$ be a field, let $m\ge n>0$, and write
\[
 A=QB+R,
 \qquad
 r=\deg R<n,
\]
with $R\ne0$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:resultant-remainder}
 \operatorname{Res}(A,B)
 =(-1)^{mn}b_n^{m-r}\operatorname{Res}(B,R).
\end{equation}
If $R=0$, both sides are interpreted as zero.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
At every root $\beta_j$ of $B$ one has $A(\beta_j)=R(\beta_j)$.  Applying
\eqref{eq:resultant-product-b} to $(A,B)$ and to $(R,B)$ gives
\[
 \operatorname{Res}(A,B)
 =(-1)^{mn}b_n^m\prod_jR(\beta_j)
\]
and
\[
 \operatorname{Res}(R,B)
 =(-1)^{rn}b_n^r\prod_jR(\beta_j).
\]
Eliminating the product and using
$\operatorname{Res}(R,B)=(-1)^{rn}\operatorname{Res}(B,R)$ yields
\eqref{eq:resultant-remainder}.  If $R=0$, then $B$ divides $A$, so the
resultant vanishes by Theorem~\ref{thm:resultant-zero-criterion}.
\end{proof}

For fraction-free arithmetic let
\[
 P=\operatorname{prem}(A,B).
\]
With $s=m-n+1$, pseudo-division satisfies
\[
 b_n^sA=QB+P.
\]
The ordinary remainder is therefore $R=P/b_n^s$ over the quotient field.

\begin{corollary}[Pseudo-remainder recurrence]
\label{cor:resultant-pseudoremainder}
Under the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:resultant-remainder}, let
$P=\operatorname{prem}(A,B)\ne0$ and put $r=\deg P$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:resultant-pseudoremainder}
 \operatorname{Res}(A,B)
 =(-1)^{mn}
  b_n^{\,m-r-n(m-n+1)}
  \operatorname{Res}(B,P).
\end{equation}
The exponent may be negative; the identity is an equality in the quotient
field, and the complete expression is nevertheless integral.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Since $P=b_n^sR$, scaling the second input gives
\[
 \operatorname{Res}(B,P)=b_n^{sn}\operatorname{Res}(B,R).
\]
Substitute this into \eqref{eq:resultant-remainder}.
\end{proof}

The negative powers in \eqref{eq:resultant-pseudoremainder} explain why a
naive resultant recurrence based directly on pseudo-remainders is awkward:
one repeatedly creates large powers only to divide them out again.  Brown's
subresultant normalization packages exactly these cancellations into known
exact factors.  The zeroth subresultant then emerges as an integer without
rational intermediate arithmetic.

\subsection{The terminal Brown invariant}

Recall the notation of Section~\ref{sec:subresultant-prs}.  Brown's PRS
produces members $F_i$ of degrees $n_i$ and auxiliary integers
\[
 h_i=\operatorname{lc}\operatorname{Sres}_{n_i}(F_1,F_2).
\]
The following observation turns the same PRS into a resultant algorithm.

\begin{theorem}[Terminal subresultant scalar]\label{thm:terminal-resultant}
Let $F_1,F_2\in\ZZ[x]$ be primitive and nonzero, with
$\deg F_1\ge\deg F_2$, and run Brown's subresultant PRS to its last nonzero
member $F_k$.
\begin{enumerate}
 \item If $\deg F_k>0$, then
 \[
  \operatorname{Res}(F_1,F_2)=0.
 \]
 \item If $\deg F_k=0$, then
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:terminal-h-resultant}
  h_k=\operatorname{Res}(F_1,F_2).
 \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
The constant polynomial $F_k$ itself need not equal the resultant.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:brown-subresultant-prs-correct},
\[
 h_k=\operatorname{lc}\operatorname{Sres}_{n_k}(F_1,F_2).
\]
If $n_k>0$, the final clause of the fundamental theorem of subresultants says
that every subresultant of index below $n_k$ vanishes.  In particular
\[
 \operatorname{Sres}_0(F_1,F_2)=0.
\]
The zeroth subresultant is the Sylvester determinant, hence the resultant.

If $n_k=0$, the displayed identity becomes
\[
 h_k=\operatorname{lc}\operatorname{Sres}_0(F_1,F_2).
\]
Since the zeroth subresultant is a constant, its leading coefficient is the
constant itself, proving \eqref{eq:terminal-h-resultant}.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[The last constant need not be the resultant]
Let
\[
 A=-2-x-5x^2,
 \qquad
 B=-3-x-5x^2.
\]
Both are primitive.  Brown's final polynomial subresultant is the constant
$5$, while its associated lower-subresultant scalar is
\[
 h=25.
\]
Since the final degree is zero, Theorem~\ref{thm:terminal-resultant} gives
\[
 \operatorname{Res}(A,B)=25.
\]
Thus returning the last PRS polynomial instead of the auxiliary $h$ would be
incorrect even on small primitive inputs.
\end{example}

The theorem also unifies the gcd, XGCD and resultant interpretations of the
same subresultant sequence.  A positive-degree terminal subresultant is a
rational associate of the gcd; a zero-degree terminal lower subresultant is
the resultant; and the bordered minors propagated in the extended sequence
supply the integral B\'ezout cofactors.

\subsection{Subresultant resultant algorithm over $\ZZ[x]$}

For arbitrary integer polynomials, contents and a possible input swap remain
to be restored.  Let
\[
 c_A=\operatorname{cont}(A),
 \qquad
 c_B=\operatorname{cont}(B),
 \qquad
 A_0=\operatorname{pp}(A),
 \qquad
 B_0=\operatorname{pp}(B).
\]
If $m=\deg A$ and $n=\deg B$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:resultant-content-law}
 \operatorname{Res}(A,B)
 =c_A^n c_B^m\operatorname{Res}(A_0,B_0).
\end{equation}

\begin{algorithm}[Subresultant resultant]
\label{alg:subresultant-resultant}
Input $A,B\in\ZZ[x]$.
\begin{enumerate}
 \item If either input is zero, return $0$.
 \item Put $m\leftarrow\deg A$, $n\leftarrow\deg B$.  If $m=n=0$, return
 $1$.  If $m=0$, return $A^n$; if $n=0$, return $B^m$.
 \item Compute $c_A,c_B,A_0,B_0$ as above.
 \item If $\deg A_0<\deg B_0$, interchange $A_0,B_0$ and remember that the
 pair was swapped.
 \item Run Brown's subresultant PRS on the ordered primitive pair, retaining
 its final nonzero member $S$ and the corresponding auxiliary scalar $h$.
 If $\deg S>0$, put $r\leftarrow0$; otherwise put $r\leftarrow h$.
 \item If the inputs were swapped and $mn$ is odd, replace $r$ by $-r$.
 \item Return
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:resultant-alg-return}
  c_A^n c_B^m r.
 \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
\end{algorithm}

\begin{theorem}[Correctness of the subresultant resultant]
\label{thm:subresultant-resultant-correct}
Algorithm~\ref{alg:subresultant-resultant} returns
$\operatorname{Res}(A,B)$ with the Sylvester sign convention
\eqref{eq:resultant-sylvester}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The constant cases are \eqref{eq:resultant-constant}, with the empty
Sylvester determinant equal to $1$ when both inputs are constants.  Assume
therefore that both degrees are positive.  Scaling law
\eqref{eq:resultant-scaling} gives
\eqref{eq:resultant-content-law}.

If the primitive inputs are already in nonincreasing degree order,
Theorem~\ref{thm:terminal-resultant} says that step 5 returns exactly
$\operatorname{Res}(A_0,B_0)$: a positive-degree last member is equivalent to
a common factor and hence zero resultant, while a constant last member makes
$h$ the zeroth subresultant.

If the primitive inputs were interchanged, equation
\eqref{eq:resultant-symmetry} shows that one must multiply the ordered
resultant by $(-1)^{mn}$.  Step 6 does exactly this.  Finally step 7 restores
the two contents by \eqref{eq:resultant-content-law}.
\end{proof}

The companion implementation uses this algorithm above a tuned small-size base
case.  For small Sylvester order $m+n$ it evaluates
\eqref{eq:resultant-sylvester} by fraction-free Bareiss elimination; above the
tuned crossover it uses Algorithm~\ref{alg:subresultant-resultant}.  This
changes only the small-degree constant factors: Bareiss uses $O(N^3)$ integer
arithmetic operations whereas the Brown PRS has the quadratic coefficient
arithmetic bound below.  The shared Brown PRS routine returns the pair $(S,h)$
from step 5; the resultant routine tests the degree of $S$ but returns $h$, not
the constant coefficient of $S$.

\subsection{Coefficient growth and complexity}

The determinant definition gives the output-size bound before any algorithm
is chosen.

\begin{theorem}[Resultant coefficient bound]\label{thm:resultant-size}
Let $A,B\in\ZZ[x]$ have degrees at most $N$ and coefficients of at most
$\tau$ bits.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:resultant-size}
 \bits\bigl(\operatorname{Res}(A,B)\bigr)
 =O\bigl(N(\tau+\log(N+1))\bigr).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The Sylvester determinant has order $m+n\le2N$.  Each row contains at most
$N+1$ nonzero input coefficients, so its Euclidean norm is at most
\[
 \sqrt{N+1}\,2^\tau.
\]
Hadamard's inequality therefore gives
\[
 |\operatorname{Res}(A,B)|
 \le
 \bigl(\sqrt{N+1}\,2^\tau\bigr)^{m+n}.
\]
Taking binary logarithms proves \eqref{eq:resultant-size}.
\end{proof}

A direct determinant computation and the subresultant method have different
polynomial arithmetic costs.  Fraction-free Gaussian elimination, or Bareiss
elimination, on the $(m+n)\times(m+n)$ Sylvester matrix uses
$O(N^3)$ integer arithmetic operations.  Brown's PRS exploits the Toeplitz-like
polynomial structure of the same determinant and needs only quadratic
coefficient arithmetic in the classical implementation.

\begin{theorem}[Coefficient complexity of the subresultant resultant]
\label{thm:resultant-coefficient-complexity}
For primitive inputs of degrees at most $N$, Algorithm~\ref{alg:subresultant-resultant}
with classical pseudo-remainders uses $O(N^2)$ coefficient additions and
multiplications and $O(N^2)$ exact coefficient divisions by known Brown
factors.  For arbitrary inputs it additionally computes two contents and
performs $O(N)$ exact scalar divisions to form the primitive parts.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The PRS computation is the same one analyzed in
Theorem~\ref{thm:subresultant-gcd-coefficient-complexity}; stopping with the
terminal pair $(S,h)$ does not add any polynomial operation.  Hence its
coefficient arithmetic and exact Brown divisions are both $O(N^2)$.  Forming
the two primitive inputs touches only $O(N)$ coefficients.  The remaining
swap, sign and content-power operations are scalar integer operations.
\end{proof}

As in the gcd computation, stored Brown-normalized remainders satisfy the
subresultant determinant bound
\[
 W=O\bigl(N(\tau+\log(N+1))\bigr),
\]
but the straightforward pseudo-remainder kernel forms a larger raw remainder
before dividing by the Brown factor.  Put
\[
 B_*=O\bigl(N(W+1)\bigr).
\]
The direct bit bound therefore parallels
Theorem~\ref{thm:subresultant-gcd-bit-complexity}.

\begin{theorem}[Direct bit complexity of the subresultant resultant]
\label{thm:resultant-bit-complexity}
With the notation above, the classical Brown-PRS resultant has bit complexity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:resultant-bit-complexity}
 O\!\left(
 N^2\bigl(\MZ(B_*)+\mathsf D_{\ZZ}(B_*)\bigr)
 +N\bigl(\mathsf G_{\ZZ}(\tau)+\mathsf D_{\ZZ}(\tau)\bigr)
 +\log(N+1)\MZ(W)
 \right).
\end{equation}
The final term covers binary powering of the two contents and multiplication
of those powers into the primitive resultant.  In the balanced nontrivial
regime it is dominated by the displayed PRS bound.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The PRS contribution is identical to the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:subresultant-gcd-bit-complexity}: there are $O(N^2)$
coefficient arithmetic operations and exact normalizing divisions on
integers of at most $O(B_*)$ bits.  The two content computations and primitive
part divisions give the second term.  Each content has at most $\tau$ bits,
and its exponent is at most $N$; the powers therefore have
$O(N(\tau+1))\subseteq O(W)$ bits.  Binary exponentiation uses
$O(\log(N+1))$ integer multiplications, and the final products with the
primitive resultant also have $O(W)$-bit operands.  This gives the last term.
\end{proof}

The resultant section therefore closes the subresultant sequence developed in
the preceding sections.  The Sylvester determinant supplies the definition
and output-size bound; Brown normalization evaluates its zeroth subresultant
without constructing the matrix; the last positive-degree subresultant gives
the gcd information; and the extended minors supply the B\'ezout certificate.
The discriminant and squarefreeness criteria are immediate applications of
this resultant machinery and will be treated next.


\section{Discriminants and squarefreeness}\label{sec:discriminant-squarefree}

The derivative and resultant together detect repeated roots.  This section
extracts two standard consequences: the discriminant, which records the
pairwise root differences in one integer, and the squarefree part, which
removes repeated irreducible factors.  Throughout, squarefreeness of an
integer polynomial means squarefreeness after extension of scalars to
$\mathbb Q[x]$; integer content is therefore ignored.  This is the convention
used by the companion implementation.

Let
\[
 f(x)=a_nx^n+\cdots+a_0\in\ZZ[x],\qquad a_n\ne0.
\]
For $n\ge1$ write $f'$ for the formal derivative.  Differentiation requires
only the scalar products
\[
 [x^{i-1}]f'=i a_i,
\]
so it costs $O(n)$ coefficient operations and increases coefficient bit size
by at most $O(\log(n+1))$.

\subsection{The discriminant}

\begin{definition}[Discriminant]\label{def:discriminant}
For $n\ge1$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:discriminant-resultant}
 \operatorname{Disc}(f)
 =(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}\frac{\operatorname{Res}(f,f')}{a_n}.
\end{equation}
The companion implementation assigns discriminant $0$ to polynomials of
length at most one, including the zero polynomial and nonzero constants.
For a linear polynomial, \eqref{eq:discriminant-resultant} gives
$\operatorname{Disc}(f)=1$.
\end{definition}

The division by $a_n$ in \eqref{eq:discriminant-resultant} is exact over
$\ZZ$.  It is useful to prove this before appealing to roots, because it
justifies the fraction-free implementation directly.

\begin{theorem}[Integrality of the discriminant]\label{thm:discriminant-integral}
For every $f\in\ZZ[x]$ of positive degree,
\[
 a_n\mid\operatorname{Res}(f,f'),
\]
so \eqref{eq:discriminant-resultant} belongs to $\ZZ$.
Indeed, as a universal expression in the coefficients,
\[
 \frac{\operatorname{Res}(f,f')}{a_n}
 \in\ZZ[a_0,\ldots,a_n].
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Regard
\[
 R(a_0,\ldots,a_n)=\operatorname{Res}(f,f')
\]
as the determinant of the $(2n-1)\times(2n-1)$ Sylvester matrix over the
polynomial ring $\ZZ[a_0,\ldots,a_n]$.  Set $a_n=0$.  Then the formal degree
of $f$ drops below $n$ and the formal degree of $f'$ drops below $n-1$.
Consequently the highest-degree column of the Sylvester matrix is zero, so
$R(a_0,\ldots,a_{n-1},0)=0$.

Viewed as a polynomial in the single indeterminate $a_n$ over
$\ZZ[a_0,\ldots,a_{n-1}]$, the constant term of $R$ is therefore zero.
Hence $a_n$ divides $R$ in the coefficient polynomial ring, proving the
claim after specialization to arbitrary integer coefficients.
\end{proof}

The root formula explains the geometry behind the definition.

\begin{theorem}[Root-difference formula]\label{thm:discriminant-root-formula}
Let $L$ be a splitting field and write
\[
 f(x)=a_n\prod_{i=1}^n(x-\alpha_i),
\]
with roots repeated according to multiplicity.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:discriminant-root-formula}
 \operatorname{Disc}(f)
 =a_n^{2n-2}\prod_{1\le i<j\le n}(\alpha_i-\alpha_j)^2.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The product formula for the resultant gives
\[
 \operatorname{Res}(f,f')
 =a_n^{n-1}\prod_{i=1}^n f'(\alpha_i).
\]
For every $i$,
\[
 f'(\alpha_i)=a_n\prod_{j\ne i}(\alpha_i-\alpha_j).
\]
Thus
\[
 \operatorname{Res}(f,f')
 =a_n^{2n-1}
   \prod_i\prod_{j\ne i}(\alpha_i-\alpha_j).
\]
Each unordered pair $\{i,j\}$ contributes
\[
 (\alpha_i-\alpha_j)(\alpha_j-\alpha_i)
 =-(\alpha_i-\alpha_j)^2.
\]
There are $n(n-1)/2$ such pairs.  Substitution into
\eqref{eq:discriminant-resultant} proves the formula.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Quadratic discriminant]
For
\[
 f=ax^2+bx+c,
\]
formula \eqref{eq:discriminant-resultant} gives
\[
 \operatorname{Disc}(f)=b^2-4ac.
\]
For example, $3x^2+2x+1$ has discriminant $-8$.
\end{example}

\begin{corollary}[Repeated-root criterion]\label{cor:discriminant-repeated-root}
For $f\in\ZZ[x]$ of positive degree,
\[
 \operatorname{Disc}(f)=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 f\text{ has a repeated root in }\overline{\mathbb Q}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \deg\gcd_{\mathbb Q[x]}(f,f')>0.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{eq:discriminant-root-formula}, the discriminant vanishes exactly
when two roots coincide.  In characteristic zero this is equivalent to a
nonconstant common factor of $f$ and $f'$.
\end{proof}

Two useful identities also follow immediately from the root formula.

\begin{proposition}[Scaling and products]\label{prop:discriminant-laws}
Let $f,g\in\ZZ[x]$ have positive degrees $m,n$, and let $c\ne0$ be an
integer.  Then
\begin{align}
 \operatorname{Disc}(cf)
 &=c^{2m-2}\operatorname{Disc}(f),
 \label{eq:discriminant-scaling}\\
 \operatorname{Disc}(fg)
 &=\operatorname{Disc}(f)\operatorname{Disc}(g)
   \operatorname{Res}(f,g)^2.
 \label{eq:discriminant-product}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Scaling changes only the leading coefficient in
\eqref{eq:discriminant-root-formula}, giving
\eqref{eq:discriminant-scaling}.  For a product, split the pairwise root
differences into pairs internal to $f$, pairs internal to $g$, and cross
pairs.  The first two groups give the two discriminants.  The cross group,
including the required leading-coefficient powers, is exactly
$\operatorname{Res}(f,g)^2$ by
Theorem~\ref{thm:resultant-product-formula}.
\end{proof}

\begin{algorithm}[Discriminant over integer polynomials]\label{alg:zz-discriminant}
Input $f\in\ZZ[x]$.
\begin{enumerate}
 \item If $\len(f)\le1$, return $0$.
 \item Put $n\leftarrow\deg f$ and compute $d\leftarrow f'$.
 \item Compute $r\leftarrow\operatorname{Res}(f,d)$.
 \item Divide $r$ exactly by $\operatorname{lc}(f)$.
 \item If $n(n-1)/2$ is odd, replace $r$ by $-r$.
 \item Return $r$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{algorithm}

\begin{theorem}[Correctness of Algorithm~\ref{alg:zz-discriminant}]
Algorithm~\ref{alg:zz-discriminant} returns the convention of
Definition~\ref{def:discriminant}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For positive degree this is exactly
\eqref{eq:discriminant-resultant}; the exactness of step 4 is
Theorem~\ref{thm:discriminant-integral}.  The low-degree branch is the stated
companion convention.
\end{proof}

The implementation tests the parity in step 5 through $n\bmod4$: the sign is
negative exactly for $n\equiv2,3\pmod4$.

\subsection{Squarefree polynomials over $\mathbb Q$}

For integer polynomials there are two possible notions of squarefreeness.  As
an element of $\ZZ[x]$, a polynomial with content divisible by $p^2$ is not
squarefree.  Polynomial algorithms usually want instead the factor structure
in $\mathbb Q[x]$, where every nonzero integer is a unit.  We use the latter
notion.

\begin{definition}[Rational squarefreeness]\label{def:rational-squarefree}
A nonzero polynomial $f\in\ZZ[x]$ is \emph{squarefree} if no nonconstant
square in $\mathbb Q[x]$ divides $f$.  Equivalently, in a factorization
\[
 f=c\prod_{i=1}^s p_i^{e_i},
 \qquad c\in\mathbb Q^\times,
\]
into pairwise nonassociate irreducibles $p_i\in\mathbb Q[x]$, every exponent
is $e_i=1$.  Nonzero constants are squarefree.  The zero polynomial is not
squarefree.
\end{definition}

Thus multiplying by a nonzero integer does not change squarefreeness.  This is
why, for example, $12(x+1)(x+2)$ is squarefree under the companion API.

\begin{theorem}[Derivative criterion]\label{thm:squarefree-derivative-criterion}
Let $f\in\ZZ[x]$ be nonzero.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:squarefree-gcd-criterion}
 f\text{ is squarefree}
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \deg\gcd_{\mathbb Q[x]}(f,f')=0.
\end{equation}
For positive degree this is also equivalent to
$\operatorname{Disc}(f)\ne0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Over $\mathbb Q[x]$, write
\[
 f=c\prod_i p_i^{e_i}.
\]
Differentiation gives
\[
 f'=c\sum_i e_i p_i^{e_i-1}p_i'
       \prod_{j\ne i}p_j^{e_j}.
\]
Because the characteristic is zero, every irreducible $p_i$ is separable and
$p_i\nmid p_i'$.  Hence the exponent of $p_i$ in $\gcd(f,f')$ is exactly
$e_i-1$.  The gcd is constant precisely when every $e_i=1$.  The
discriminant equivalence is Corollary~\ref{cor:discriminant-repeated-root}.
\end{proof}

The canonical gcd over $\ZZ[x]$ may contain a nonunit integer content, but
that does not affect the degree test.  If $f=cF$ with $c=\operatorname{cont}f$
and $F=\operatorname{pp}(f)$, then
\[
 \gcd_{\ZZ[x]}(f,f')
 =c\,H
\]
for a primitive polynomial $H$ associated over $\mathbb Q[x]$ to
$\gcd(F,F')$.  Thus checking whether the integer-polynomial gcd has length at
most one implements \eqref{eq:squarefree-gcd-criterion} exactly.

\begin{algorithm}[Squarefree test]\label{alg:is-squarefree}
Input $f\in\ZZ[x]$.
\begin{enumerate}
 \item If $f=0$, return false.
 \item If $\deg f\le1$, return true.
 \item Compute $d\leftarrow f'$ and $g\leftarrow\gcd_{\ZZ[x]}(f,d)$.
 \item Return true if and only if $\deg g=0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{algorithm}

\begin{theorem}[Correctness of Algorithm~\ref{alg:is-squarefree}]
Algorithm~\ref{alg:is-squarefree} returns true exactly for the squarefree
polynomials of Definition~\ref{def:rational-squarefree}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The zero, constant and linear cases agree with the definition.  For degree at
least two, the integer gcd and rational gcd have the same polynomial degree,
so the result follows from Theorem~\ref{thm:squarefree-derivative-criterion}.
\end{proof}

Although the discriminant gives another squarefree test, the companion
implementation uses the gcd criterion.  This avoids constructing the possibly
large scalar resultant when only the existence of a repeated factor is
required.

\subsection{The squarefree part}

The quotient by the derivative gcd removes precisely the excess copies of
each irreducible factor.

\begin{definition}[Squarefree part]\label{def:squarefree-part}
For nonzero $f\in\ZZ[x]$, let
\[
 G=\gcd_{\ZZ[x]}(f,f')
\]
with the canonical positive-leading-coefficient convention.  Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:squarefree-part}
 \operatorname{sqfpart}(f)
 =\operatorname{pos}\!\left(\frac{f}{G}\right),
\end{equation}
where $\operatorname{pos}(q)$ is $q$ or $-q$ according as its leading
coefficient is positive or negative.  Set
$\operatorname{sqfpart}(0)=0$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Structure of the squarefree part]
\label{thm:squarefree-part-structure}
Let $f\ne0$ and factor its primitive part over $\mathbb Q[x]$ as
\[
 \operatorname{pp}(f)=u\prod_{i=1}^s p_i^{e_i},
\]
where the $p_i$ are pairwise nonassociate primitive irreducible integer
polynomials and the unit $u\in\{\pm1\}$ accounts for sign.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:squarefree-part-factorization}
 \operatorname{sqfpart}(f)
 =\operatorname{pos}\!\left(u\prod_{i=1}^s p_i\right).
\end{equation}
In particular, $\operatorname{sqfpart}(f)$ is primitive, has positive leading
coefficient, is squarefree, and has exactly the distinct roots of $f$.
For a nonzero constant, $\operatorname{sqfpart}(f)=1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:squarefree-derivative-criterion} shows that the
primitive polynomial part of $\gcd(f,f')$ contains $p_i^{e_i-1}$ for every
$i$.  Moreover, the common integer content of $f$ and $f'$ contains the full
positive content of $f$, so dividing by the canonical integer-polynomial gcd
removes the content as well.  Therefore $f/G$ is, up to sign, exactly the
primitive product $\prod_i p_i$.  Gauss's lemma makes this quotient primitive.
The final sign normalization gives
\eqref{eq:squarefree-part-factorization}.  The constant case is the empty
product.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Radical ideal interpretation]
For nonzero $f\in\ZZ[x]$,
\[
 \sqrt{(f)\mathbb Q[x]}
 =(\operatorname{sqfpart}(f))\mathbb Q[x].
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Both ideals are generated, up to a rational unit, by the product of the
distinct irreducible factors of $f$.
\end{proof}

\begin{algorithm}[Squarefree part over integer polynomials]\label{alg:squarefree-part}
Input $f\in\ZZ[x]$.
\begin{enumerate}
 \item If $f=0$, return $0$.
 \item Compute $d\leftarrow f'$ and $g\leftarrow\gcd_{\ZZ[x]}(f,d)$.
 \item Compute the exact quotient $q\leftarrow f/g$.
 \item If $\operatorname{lc}(q)<0$, replace $q$ by $-q$.
 \item Return $q$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{algorithm}

\begin{theorem}[Correctness of the squarefree-part algorithm]
Algorithm~\ref{alg:squarefree-part} returns
$\operatorname{sqfpart}(f)$ from Definition~\ref{def:squarefree-part}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $f=0$ this is the chosen convention.  Otherwise the quotient in step 3 is
exact by definition of the gcd, and Theorem~\ref{thm:squarefree-part-structure}
identifies it up to sign with the product of the distinct primitive factors.
Step 4 imposes the canonical positive-leading-coefficient normalization.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Coefficient growth and complexity}

Discriminants inherit both their size and their computational cost from the
resultant.  If $f$ has degree at most $N$ and input coefficients of at most
$\tau$ bits, then the coefficients of $f'$ have
$O(\tau+\log(N+1))$ bits.  The resultant bound from
Theorem~\ref{thm:resultant-size} immediately yields the following.

\begin{theorem}[Discriminant size]\label{thm:discriminant-size}
For $f\in\ZZ[x]$ of degree at most $N$ and coefficient bit size at most
$\tau$,
\[
 \bits(\operatorname{Disc}(f))
 =O\bigl(N(\tau+\log(N+1))\bigr).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:resultant-size} to $(f,f')$.  Exact division by the
nonzero leading coefficient cannot increase the absolute value when
$|a_n|\ge1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Coefficient complexity]\label{thm:disc-squarefree-coefficient-complexity}
With classical Brown-PRS gcd and resultant algorithms, for input degree at
most $N$:
\begin{enumerate}
 \item the discriminant uses $O(N^2)$ polynomial coefficient operations;
 \item the squarefree test uses $O(N^2)$ polynomial coefficient operations;
 \item the squarefree part uses $O(N^2)$ polynomial coefficient operations
 in addition to one exact polynomial division.
\end{enumerate}
With a classical exact division, all three bounds are $O(N^2)$ coefficient
operations.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Differentiation is linear.  The first claim then follows from
Theorem~\ref{thm:resultant-coefficient-complexity}; the final division by the
leading coefficient is one scalar exact division.  The second follows from
Theorem~\ref{thm:subresultant-gcd-coefficient-complexity}.  The third performs
the same gcd and then an exact quotient of degree at most $N$.
\end{proof}

For bit complexity, the direct bounds from the preceding sections may be
reused with the derivative coefficient size
$\tau'=O(\tau+\log(N+1))$.  Thus the discriminant has the bit cost of the
Brown-PRS resultant on $(f,f')$, followed by one exact division of an
$O(N(\tau+\log N))$-bit integer by the leading coefficient.  The squarefree
test has the corresponding Brown-PRS gcd cost.  The squarefree part adds the
bit cost of one exact polynomial quotient.  As before, these direct bounds
include the raw pseudo-remainders formed before Brown normalization; the
stored normalized PRS coefficients themselves satisfy determinant-size
bounds.

The resulting hierarchy is therefore simple.  The discriminant is the scalar
certificate of repeated roots, the derivative gcd identifies their common
polynomial factor, and the squarefree part removes all excess multiplicities.
All three operations reuse the resultant, gcd and exact-division machinery
already developed in this chapter.


\chapter{Fourier transforms and transform multiplication}
\label{chap:fft}

Fast transform multiplication replaces coefficient convolution by pointwise
multiplication after evaluation at roots of unity.  The transform itself is
independent of whether the roots live in a finite field or in a composite
residue ring.  This chapter first develops the common algebra, then gives two
exact specializations for integer polynomials: number-theoretic transforms
combined by the Chinese remainder theorem, and a single transform modulo a
number of the form $2^K+1$.

The radix-$2$ factorization is the classical FFT of Cooley and Tukey
\cite{CooleyTukey1965}.  Pollard \cite{Pollard1971} developed the finite-field
version for exact convolution.  The use of the rings $\ZZ/(2^K+1)\ZZ$, where
powers of two are especially cheap roots of unity, is central to the
Sch\"onhage--Strassen multiplication method \cite{SchonhageStrassen1971}.
Brent and Zimmermann \cite{BrentZimmermann2010} give a modern treatment of
modular FFT arithmetic.

\section{Discrete Fourier transforms over rings}

Let $R$ be a commutative ring with identity and let $N\geq1$.  The form needed
below is slightly more general than a transform over a field.

\begin{definition}[Principal root of unity]
An element $\omega\in R$ is a \emph{principal $N$th root of unity} if
$\omega^N=1$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:principal-root-orthogonality}
 \sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\omega^{ij}=0,
 \qquad 1\leq i<N.
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

If $R$ is a field, every element of exact order $N$ is principal: for
$1\leq i<N$, the geometric sum in
\eqref{eq:principal-root-orthogonality} is annihilated by the nonzero element
$\omega^i-1$.  Over a ring with zero divisors, exact multiplicative order alone
is not sufficient, which is why the orthogonality condition is stated
explicitly.

\begin{definition}[Discrete Fourier transform]
Let $\omega$ be a principal $N$th root in $R$.  For
$a=(a_0,\ldots,a_{N-1})\in R^N$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dft-definition}
 \widehat a_k=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}a_j\omega^{jk},
 \qquad 0\leq k<N.
\end{equation}
The map $a\mapsto\widehat a$ is denoted $\mathcal F_\omega$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Fourier inversion]\label{thm:ring-dft-inversion}
Suppose that $\omega$ is a principal $N$th root in $R$ and that $N$ is a unit
in $R$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dft-inverse}
 a_j=N^{-1}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\widehat a_k\omega^{-jk}.
\end{equation}
Thus $\mathcal F_\omega^{-1}=N^{-1}\mathcal F_{\omega^{-1}}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Substituting \eqref{eq:dft-definition} into the right side of
\eqref{eq:dft-inverse} gives
\[
 N^{-1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{N-1}a_\ell
 \sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\omega^{(\ell-j)k}.
\]
If $\ell=j$, the inner sum is $N$.  Otherwise
$\ell-j\not\equiv0\pmod N$, so the inner sum is zero by
\eqref{eq:principal-root-orthogonality}.  Only $a_j$ remains.
\end{proof}

For $a,b\in R^N$, define their cyclic convolution by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cyclic-convolution}
 (a*b)_k=\sum_{i+j\equiv k\pmod N}a_i b_j.
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Convolution theorem]\label{thm:ring-convolution}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:ring-dft-inversion},
\[
 \mathcal F_\omega(a*b)_k=\widehat a_k\widehat b_k
 \qquad(0\leq k<N).
\]
Consequently cyclic convolution is obtained by two forward transforms,
$N$ pointwise products, and one inverse transform.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Expanding the transform of \eqref{eq:cyclic-convolution}, and writing
$k\equiv i+j\pmod N$, gives
\[
 \sum_{i,j}a_i b_j\omega^{(i+j)\ell}
 =\left(\sum_i a_i\omega^{i\ell}\right)
  \left(\sum_j b_j\omega^{j\ell}\right).
\]
This is $\widehat a_\ell\widehat b_\ell$.  Fourier inversion gives the final
statement.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Ordinary polynomial product]\label{cor:zero-padded-convolution}
Let $f,g\in R[x]$ have lengths $m,n$, and let $N\geq m+n-1$.  Pad their
coefficient vectors with zeros to length $N$.  Their cyclic convolution is the
coefficient vector of the ordinary product $fg$ followed by zeros.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Every nonzero product term has index $i+j\leq m+n-2<N$, so reduction of the
index modulo $N$ never wraps around.
\end{proof}

\section{The radix-$2$ FFT}

Assume now that $N=2^r$ and $\omega$ is a principal $N$th root.  A direct DFT
uses $N^2$ ring operations.  The radix-$2$ factorization reduces this to
$O(N\log N)$.

Put $h=N/2$.  For $0\leq j<h$, set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dif-butterfly}
 u_j=a_j+a_{j+h},
 \qquad
 v_j=(a_j-a_{j+h})\omega^j.
\end{equation}
For even and odd frequency indices respectively,
\begin{align}
 \widehat a_{2k}
   &=\sum_{j=0}^{h-1}u_j(\omega^2)^{jk},\label{eq:dif-even}\\
 \widehat a_{2k+1}
   &=\sum_{j=0}^{h-1}v_j(\omega^2)^{jk}.\label{eq:dif-odd}
\end{align}
Thus one length-$N$ transform reduces to two length-$h$ transforms after
$h$ butterflies.  This is the decimation-in-frequency form.

\begin{algorithm}[Radix-$2$ forward DIF transform]\label{alg:fft-dif}
Input $a_0,\ldots,a_{N-1}$ and a principal $N$th root $\omega$.
For $m=N,N/2,\ldots,2$, put $h=m/2$ and $s=N/m$.  For each block beginning
at $b=0,m,2m,\ldots,N-m$ and each $0\leq j<h$, replace
\[
 (a_{b+j},a_{b+j+h})
 \longleftarrow
 \bigl(a_{b+j}+a_{b+j+h},
       (a_{b+j}-a_{b+j+h})\omega^{js}\bigr).
\]
The resulting entries are $\widehat a_k$ in bit-reversed order.
\end{algorithm}

The inverse can use the reverse scheduling.  Its elementary DIT butterfly is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dit-inverse-butterfly}
 (x,y)\longmapsto
 \bigl(x+\omega^{-e}y,\ x-\omega^{-e}y\bigr).
\end{equation}
After all stages every entry is multiplied by $N^{-1}$.

\begin{algorithm}[Radix-$2$ inverse DIT transform]\label{alg:fft-dit-inverse}
Input the Fourier values in bit-reversed order.  For
$m=2,4,\ldots,N$, put $h=m/2$ and $s=N/m$.  For each block beginning at
$b=0,m,2m,\ldots,N-m$ and $0\leq j<h$, replace
\[
 (a_{b+j},a_{b+j+h})
 \longleftarrow
 \bigl(a_{b+j}+\omega^{-js}a_{b+j+h},
       a_{b+j}-\omega^{-js}a_{b+j+h}\bigr).
\]
Finally multiply every entry by $N^{-1}$.  The output is in natural order.
\end{algorithm}

\begin{theorem}[Correctness of the radix-$2$ pair]
Algorithm~\ref{alg:fft-dif} computes $\mathcal F_\omega$, with bit-reversed
output ordering.  Algorithm~\ref{alg:fft-dit-inverse} consumes that ordering
and computes $\mathcal F_\omega^{-1}$ in natural order.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Equations \eqref{eq:dif-even} and \eqref{eq:dif-odd} prove one DIF stage.
Recursing first on the even-frequency half and then on the odd-frequency half
moves the low frequency bit to the outside at each stage, hence reverses the
$r$ frequency bits.  Repeating the factorization for all $r$ stages proves the
forward claim.

By Theorem~\ref{thm:ring-dft-inversion}, the inverse transform is
$N^{-1}\mathcal F_{\omega^{-1}}$.  Reversing the forward factorization gives
the DIT butterflies \eqref{eq:dit-inverse-butterfly}; it consumes the
bit-reversed order produced by DIF and restores natural order.  The final
factor $N^{-1}$ completes Fourier inversion.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Radix-$2$ operation count]\label{thm:fft-operation-count}
A length-$N=2^r$ radix-$2$ transform has $r$ stages and $N/2$ butterflies per
stage.  Hence it uses exactly $Nr/2$ butterflies, $Nr$ additions or
subtractions, and at most $Nr/2$ multiplications by roots of unity.  The
inverse has the same cost plus $N$ normalizations by $N^{-1}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Each stage partitions the $N$ entries into $N/m$ blocks, with $m/2$
butterflies per block, hence $N/2$ butterflies.  There are $r=\log_2N$
stages.  Each butterfly has two additions or subtractions and at most one
twiddle multiplication.
\end{proof}

\section{A cache-friendly matrix Fourier algorithm}
\label{sec:mfa}

The radix-$2$ FFT fixes the arithmetic count, but not the order in which the
butterflies are visited.  For large ring elements this order matters because a
long transform repeatedly streams through data that no longer fits in cache.
The matrix Fourier algorithm (MFA) reorganizes the same transform into shorter
column and row transforms.  It has the same arithmetic and bit complexity as
the radix-$2$ FFT, but usually has better locality.  More importantly for the
next stage of the implementation, the same decomposition underlies the
cache-friendly TFT and ITFT of Harvey \cite{Harvey2008CacheTFT}.

The convenient recursive object is a weighted transform whose output is stored
in bit-reversed order.  Let $L=2^\ell$, let $\omega_L$ be a principal $L$th
root of unity, let $\zeta$ be a unit, and write $j'$ for the $\ell$-bit
reversal of $j$.

\begin{definition}[Weighted bit-reversed transform]\label{def:weighted-dft}
For $a=(a_0,\ldots,a_{L-1})$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:weighted-dft}
 \mathcal F_{L,\zeta}(a)_j
 =\zeta^{j'}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\omega_L^{ij'}a_i,
 \qquad 0\leq j<L.
\end{equation}
Thus $\mathcal F_{L,1}(a)_j$ is the ordinary Fourier coefficient with
frequency $j'$.  The case $\zeta\ne1$ occurs naturally in the column
transforms below and later in the TFT.
\end{definition}

The weighted transform needs no extra multiplication per butterfly.  In a DIF
butterfly, if the current root exponent is $e$, one computes
\begin{equation}\label{eq:weighted-dif-butterfly}
 (x,y)\longmapsto
 \bigl(x+y,(x-y)\omega^e\bigr),
\end{equation}
and the weight is simply included in $e$.  This is the weighted radix-$2$
kernel used at the leaves of the implementation.

Suppose now that $L=L_1L_2$, where $L_i=2^{\ell_i}$ and
$\ell_1+\ell_2=\ell$.  Write
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mfa-index-split}
 i=i_2+L_2i_1,
 \qquad
 j=j_2+L_2j_1,
\end{equation}
with $0\leq i_1,j_1<L_1$ and $0\leq i_2,j_2<L_2$.

\begin{lemma}[Bit reversal under a matrix split]\label{lem:mfa-bit-reversal}
With the notation above,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mfa-bit-reversal}
 j'=j_1'+L_1j_2',
\end{equation}
where $j_1'$ and $j_2'$ are the $\ell_1$- and $\ell_2$-bit reversals of
$j_1$ and $j_2$ respectively.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The binary expansion of $j$ consists of the $\ell_1$ bits of $j_1$ followed
by the $\ell_2$ bits of $j_2$, with $j_2$ occupying the low positions.
Reversing all $\ell$ bits reverses each block and exchanges their order.
Thus the low $\ell_1$ bits of $j'$ are $j_1'$ and the remaining bits are
$j_2'$, which is exactly \eqref{eq:mfa-bit-reversal}.
\end{proof}

Regard the input as an $L_1\times L_2$ matrix in row-major order.  For each
column $k_2$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mfa-columns}
 b_{k_2+L_2k_1}
 = (\zeta\omega_L^{k_2})^{k_1'}
   \sum_{m=0}^{L_1-1}\omega_{L_1}^{mk_1'}
   a_{k_2+L_2m}.
\end{equation}
Thus column $k_2$ is a length-$L_1$ weighted transform with weight
$\zeta\omega_L^{k_2}$.  Next, for every row $j_1$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mfa-rows}
 c_{j_2+L_2j_1}
 = (\zeta^{L_1})^{j_2'}
   \sum_{r=0}^{L_2-1}\omega_{L_2}^{rj_2'}
   b_{r+L_2j_1}.
\end{equation}
Each row is therefore a length-$L_2$ weighted transform with the common
weight $\zeta^{L_1}$.

\begin{algorithm}[Recursive matrix Fourier transform]\label{alg:mfa-forward}
To compute $\mathcal F_{L,\zeta}$ in place:
\begin{enumerate}
 \item if $L$ is at most the chosen leaf size, use the weighted radix-$2$
 kernel \eqref{eq:weighted-dif-butterfly};
 \item otherwise choose
 $L_1=2^{\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor}$ and
 $L_2=2^{\lceil\ell/2\rceil}$;
 \item apply $\mathcal F_{L_1,\zeta\omega_L^{k_2}}$ to each of the $L_2$
 strided columns, producing the values in \eqref{eq:mfa-columns};
 \item apply $\mathcal F_{L_2,\zeta^{L_1}}$ to each of the $L_1$ contiguous
 rows, producing the values in \eqref{eq:mfa-rows}.
\end{enumerate}
The inverse performs the row transforms first and the column transforms
second, with inverse roots and inverse weights, and multiplies by $L^{-1}$
once at the end.
\end{algorithm}

\begin{theorem}[Correctness of the MFA]\label{thm:mfa}
Algorithm~\ref{alg:mfa-forward} computes the weighted transform
\eqref{eq:weighted-dft}.  In particular, when $\zeta=1$ it computes the
ordinary DFT in exactly the same bit-reversed ordering as
Algorithm~\ref{alg:fft-dif}.  The inverse described in
Algorithm~\ref{alg:mfa-forward} recovers the input in natural order.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix $j=j_2+L_2j_1$.  Substitute \eqref{eq:mfa-columns} into
\eqref{eq:mfa-rows}.  Since
$\omega_{L_1}=\omega_L^{L_2}$ and
$\omega_{L_2}=\omega_L^{L_1}$, the power of $\zeta$ is
\[
 \zeta^{k_1'+L_1j_2'},
\]
where the row index $k_1$ is $j_1$.  By
Lemma~\ref{lem:mfa-bit-reversal}, this is $\zeta^{j'}$.

The exponent of $\omega_L$ attached to the term
$a_{i_2+L_2i_1}$ is
\[
 L_2i_1j_1'+i_2j_1'+L_1i_2j_2'.
\]
On the other hand,
\begin{align*}
 (i_2+L_2i_1)(j_1'+L_1j_2')
  ={}&L_2i_1j_1'+i_2j_1'+L_1i_2j_2'\\
    &{}+Li_1j_2'.
\end{align*}
The final term is a multiple of $L$, so it disappears because
$\omega_L^L=1$.  Therefore the exponent is congruent to $ij'$ modulo $L$,
and \eqref{eq:mfa-rows} becomes
\[
 c_j=\zeta^{j'}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\omega_L^{ij'}a_i.
\]
This is exactly \eqref{eq:weighted-dft}.

The recursive calls compute precisely the column and row transforms just
proved, so induction on $\ell$ establishes the forward algorithm.  Each
weighted transform is invertible: it is an ordinary DFT, a bit-reversal
permutation and multiplication by units.  Reversing the row and column
stages with inverse roots and inverse weights therefore gives the inverse.
The two unnormalized inverse dimensions contribute the factor
$L_1L_2=L$, so one final multiplication by $L^{-1}$ completes inversion.
\end{proof}

The implementation carries a data stride, a root stride and the exponent of
$\zeta$ through the recursion rather than materializing columns or separate
root tables.  Small subtransforms use the weighted radix-$2$ kernel.  This is
important for the planned TFT: partially populated rows and columns require
exactly the same weighted, strided transforms, so the MFA is not a temporary
cache optimization that must later be replaced.

\begin{theorem}[MFA arithmetic count]\label{thm:mfa-operation-count}
A complete length-$L=2^\ell$ MFA uses exactly $L\ell/2$ radix-$2$
butterflies.  Consequently it uses exactly $L\ell$ additions or
subtractions and at most $L\ell/2$ multiplications by roots of unity, as in
Theorem~\ref{thm:fft-operation-count}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $B(L)$ be the butterfly count.  For $L=L_1L_2$, the column stage consists
of $L_2$ transforms of length $L_1$, and the row stage consists of $L_1$
transforms of length $L_2$.  Hence
\[
 B(L)=L_2B(L_1)+L_1B(L_2).
\]
Using the radix-$2$ leaf count and induction,
\begin{align*}
 B(L)
 &=\frac{L_2L_1\log_2L_1}{2}
   +\frac{L_1L_2\log_2L_2}{2}\\
 &=\frac{L}{2}(\log_2L_1+\log_2L_2)
  =\frac{L\log_2L}{2}.
\end{align*}
A weighted butterfly still has two additions or subtractions and at most one
multiplication by a root power, since the weight is absorbed into the twiddle
exponent.  A multiplication by $1$ may of course be omitted.  Thus the same
upper bound holds for the root multiplications.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[MFA bit complexity]\label{thm:mfa-bit-complexity}
Suppose a transform ring element occupies $O(K)$ bits, addition and
subtraction cost $O(K)$ bit operations, and multiplication by every root power
used by the transform costs $O(K)$ bit operations.  Then a length-$L$ MFA has
bit complexity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mfa-bit-complexity}
 O(KL\log L)
\end{equation}
and uses $O(KL)$ bits of transform storage.  In particular, replacing the
radix-$2$ transforms in the $2^K+1$ multiplication algorithm by MFA transforms
does not change the bound
\[
 O\bigl(NK\log N+N\MZ(K)\bigr)
\]
from Theorem~\ref{thm:ssa-poly-complexity}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{thm:mfa-operation-count} gives $O(L\log L)$ butterflies, and
under the stated hypotheses each butterfly costs $O(K)$ bit operations.
The transform remains in place, so its array contains $L$ ring elements of
$O(K)$ bits.  Only a constant number of temporary ring elements and a
logarithmic recursion stack are needed in addition.

For the ring $\ZZ/(2^K+1)\ZZ$, all transform roots are powers of two.
Multiplication by such a root is a shift followed by signed folding modulo
$2^K+1$, hence costs $O(K)$ bit operations.  The two forward transforms and
one inverse therefore still cost $O(NK\log N)$.  The $N$ pointwise products
are unchanged and still cost $O(N\MZ(K))$.
\end{proof}

Thus the MFA does not improve the asymptotic bit complexity, nor does it save
any butterflies in a full transform.  Its advantage is locality: the balanced
choice $L_1\approx L_2\approx\sqrt L$ turns a long transform into families of
short transforms whose working sets can fit lower levels of cache.  The
machine-dependent crossover is therefore tuned rather than fixed.  This also
explains why the MFA is a useful stepping stone rather than the final
smoothing mechanism: the later TFT will use the same matrix decomposition but
will omit work belonging to known zero inputs and unrequested outputs.

\section{Truncated Fourier transforms}
\label{sec:tft}

The full MFA improves locality but still computes all $L$ Fourier values even
when a product has only $n<L$ coefficients.  The truncated Fourier transform
(TFT) removes this remaining padding work.  We use the strict, cache-friendly
form of Harvey \cite{Harvey2008CacheTFT}, with the same weighted bit-reversed
transform as Definition~\ref{def:weighted-dft}.

\begin{definition}[Truncated Fourier transform]
\label{def:tft}
Let $1\leq z,n\leq L$, suppose $a_z=\cdots=a_{L-1}=0$, and write
$\widehat a=\mathcal F_{L,\zeta}(a)$.  The transform
$\operatorname{TFT}(L,\zeta,z,n)$ takes the initial data
$a_0,\ldots,a_{z-1}$ and returns only
$\widehat a_0,\ldots,\widehat a_{n-1}$.
\end{definition}

The omitted input cells need not actually contain zero.  The algorithm simply
never reads a cell that represents one of the known zeros.  This matters in an
in-place implementation because the unused cells are available as temporary
storage.

Let $L=L_1L_2$ be the balanced split from Section~\ref{sec:mfa}.  Decompose
\[
 n=n_2+L_2n_1,\qquad z=z_2+L_2z_1,
\]
where $0\leq n_2,z_2<L_2$, and put
\[
 n_1'=n_1+[n_2>0],\qquad
 z_2'=\begin{cases}L_2,&z_1>0,\\ z_2,&z_1=0.\end{cases}
\]
Here brackets denote an indicator.  The first $z_2$ columns contain $z_1+1$
nonzero entries, the remaining columns below $z_2'$ contain $z_1$, and all
columns from $z_2'$ onward are identically zero when $z_1=0$.

\begin{algorithm}[Cache-friendly TFT]\label{alg:tft}
For $L=2$, compute only the requested parts of
\[
 (a_0+a_1,\ \zeta(a_0-a_1)),
\]
treating $a_1$ as zero when $z=1$.  For $L>2$:
\begin{enumerate}
 \item for $0\leq u<z_2$, apply
 $\operatorname{TFT}(L_1,\zeta\omega_L^u,z_1+1,n_1')$ to column $u$;
 \item for $z_2\leq u<z_2'$, apply
 $\operatorname{TFT}(L_1,\zeta\omega_L^u,z_1,n_1')$ to column $u$;
 \item apply $\operatorname{TFT}(L_2,\zeta^{L_1},z_2',L_2)$ to each of the
 first $n_1$ rows;
 \item if $n_2>0$, apply
 $\operatorname{TFT}(L_2,\zeta^{L_1},z_2',n_2)$ to row $n_1$.
\end{enumerate}
A recursive call with $z=n=L$ is an ordinary weighted MFA and is delegated to
that implementation.
\end{algorithm}

\begin{theorem}[Correctness of the TFT]\label{thm:tft-correct}
Algorithm~\ref{alg:tft} returns exactly the first $n$ entries of
$\mathcal F_{L,\zeta}(a)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The assertion for $L=2$ is immediate.  For the recursive case use the MFA
factorization \eqref{eq:mfa-columns}--\eqref{eq:mfa-rows}.  Column $u<z_2$
has $z_1+1$ possibly nonzero inputs, while a column with
$z_2\leq u<z_2'$ has $z_1$.  Thus the first two steps compute exactly the
entries $b_{u+L_2v}$ needed for $0\leq v<n_1'$ and $0\leq u<z_2'$.
If $z_1=0$, every omitted column is identically zero; if $z_1>0$, then
$z_2'=L_2$ and there are no omitted columns.

Consequently each of the first $n_1$ rows has precisely the input prefix
needed for its complete row transform, and the next row has precisely the
input prefix needed for its first $n_2$ outputs.  Equation
\eqref{eq:mfa-rows} identifies these values with
$\widehat a_0,\ldots,\widehat a_{n-1}$.  Induction on $\log_2L$ proves the
claim.
\end{proof}

The inverse needs a slightly richer interface because the missing Fourier
values cannot simply be declared zero.

\begin{definition}[Inverse truncated Fourier transform]
\label{def:itft}
Let $f\in\{0,1\}$, $z\geq n$, $1\leq z\leq L$ and
$1\leq n+f\leq L$.  Suppose $a_z=\cdots=a_{L-1}=0$.  The ITFT receives
\[
 \widehat a_0,\ldots,\widehat a_{n-1},
 La_n,\ldots,La_{z-1}
\]
in the first $z$ cells.  It returns $La_0,\ldots,La_{n-1}$ and, when $f=1$,
also $\widehat a_n$.
\end{definition}

The factors $L$ make the recursion fraction-free apart from exact divisions by
two in the $L=2$ base case.  For polynomial multiplication we need only the
specialization $z=n$, $f=0$; after the ITFT we divide the first $n$ entries by
$L$.

For completeness, the recursive schedule used by the implementation is given
next.  Write $m=\min(n_2,z_2)$, $m'=\max(n_2,z_2)$ and
$f'=[n_2+f>0]$.

\begin{algorithm}[Cache-friendly ITFT]\label{alg:itft}
For $L=2$, solve the two-point transform directly.  For $L>2$:
\begin{enumerate}
 \item apply full ITFTs to rows $0,\ldots,n_1-1$;
 \item for $n_2\leq u<m'$, apply the column ITFT with parameters
 $(z_1+1,n_1,f')$, and for $m'\leq u<z_2'$ use $(z_1,n_1,f')$;
 \item if $f'=1$, apply the row ITFT with parameters $(z_2',n_2,f)$ to row
 $n_1$;
 \item for $0\leq u<m$, apply the column ITFT with parameters
 $(z_1+1,n_1+1,0)$, and for $m\leq u<n_2$ use $(z_1,n_1+1,0)$.
\end{enumerate}
The row weight is $\zeta^{L_1}$ and column $u$ has weight
$\zeta\omega_L^u$, exactly as in the MFA.
\end{algorithm}

\begin{theorem}[Correctness of the ITFT]\label{thm:itft-correct}
Algorithm~\ref{alg:itft} computes the outputs in
Definition~\ref{def:itft}.  Hence the specialization $z=n$, $f=0$, followed
by division by $L$, is inverse to the length-$n$ TFT on data supported in
$0,\ldots,n-1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For $L=2$, write
\[
 \widehat a_0=a_0+a_1,\qquad
 \widehat a_1=\zeta(a_0-a_1).
\]
The seven possible input configurations give the stated outputs by one addition
or subtraction and, where necessary, multiplication by $\zeta^{\pm1}$ or
exact division by two.

For $L>2$, use the same matrix factorization as in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:tft-correct}.  The first phase inverts every complete known
Fourier row, producing the corresponding scaled intermediate $b$-values.
The right-hand column phase then recovers exactly the intermediate entries
needed to reconstruct the possibly partial row $n_1$.  That row ITFT supplies
the remaining Fourier datum when $f=1$ and the intermediate values needed on
the left.  Finally the left-hand column phase recovers
$La_0,\ldots,La_{n-1}$.  The split at $m=\min(n_2,z_2)$ distinguishes columns
that contain the extra coefficient $a_{z_1L_2+u}$ from those that do not; the
split at $m'=\max(n_2,z_2)$ distinguishes the columns required before the last
row can be inverted.  Thus every recursive call receives exactly the mixed
Fourier/coefficient data specified by Definition~\ref{def:itft}.

Each recursive dimension contributes its own unnormalized factor, $L_2$ on a
row and $L_1$ on a column, so their product is $L$.  Induction therefore gives
precisely the scaling asserted in the definition.  This is also the invariant
used in Harvey's proof \cite{Harvey2008CacheTFT}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[TFT/ITFT complexity]\label{thm:tft-complexity}
A length-$L=2^\ell$ TFT requesting $n$ outputs, and the corresponding ITFT,
use $O(n\ell+L)$ radix-$2$ butterfly work.  More precisely, the cache-friendly
algorithms execute at most $\ell n/2+L$ two-point base operations.  If ring
elements occupy $O(K)$ bits and root multiplication costs $O(K)$, each
transform therefore costs
\[
 O\bigl(K(n\log L+L)\bigr)
\]
bit operations and $O(KL)$ bits of storage.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The exact base-operation bound is the truncation count proved for the strict
cache-friendly TFT and ITFT in \cite{Harvey2008CacheTFT}: a complete transform
has $L\ell/2$ butterflies, while pruning the unrequested output region leaves
at most $\ell n/2+L$ two-point leaves.  The matrix rearrangement changes only
the visitation order, so it has the same count.  Multiplying the arithmetic
count by the $O(K)$ cost of a butterfly gives the bit bound.  The in-place
array still has $L$ ring elements.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Truncated transform multiplication]
\label{cor:tft-multiplication}
Let $g,h$ have lengths $z_g,z_h$, put $n=z_g+z_h-1$, and let $L$ be the least
power of two at least $n$.  Two TFTs requesting $n$ outputs, $n$ pointwise
products, and an ITFT with $z=n$, $f=0$ recover $gh$.  In the $2^K+1$ ring the
bit cost is
\[
 O\bigl(K(n\log L+L)+n\MZ(K)\bigr).
\]
Since $L<2n$ unless $n=1$, this has the same asymptotic bound as the padded
FFT method, but the transform and pointwise work now varies with $n$ rather
than jumping immediately to the full length $L$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The two TFTs give the first $n$ Fourier coordinates of the zero-padded inputs.
Pointwise multiplication therefore gives the first $n$ Fourier coordinates of
$gh$.  The product has no coefficients from index $n$ onward, so
Theorem~\ref{thm:itft-correct} with $z=n$, $f=0$ recovers its coefficients.
The complexity follows from Theorem~\ref{thm:tft-complexity} and the $n$
pointwise products.
\end{proof}

The implementation uses this truncated round trip in both the CRT-NTT and SSA
multipliers whenever $n<L$.  At the exact power-of-two endpoints $n=L$ it
keeps the existing full transform path, including the tuned radix-$2$/MFA
choice for SSA.  Thus the new code removes padding work without perturbing the
already tuned endpoint implementation.

\section{Middle products by cyclic wraparound}
\label{sec:fft-middle-wrap}

The convolution theorem can also accelerate the balanced middle product without
computing the complete product.  The useful observation is that cyclic
wraparound lands entirely below the desired middle window.

Let $a$ have formal length $2n-1$, let $b$ have formal length $n$, and write
\[
 a(x)b(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{3n-3}c_kx^k.
\]
Choose an FFT length $L\geq2n-1$, and let
\[
 r(x)\equiv a(x)b(x)\pmod{x^L-1},\qquad \deg r<L.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Wraparound middle-product identity]
\label{thm:fft-middle-wrap}
For $n-1\leq k\leq2n-2$ one has
\[
 [x^k]r=c_k.
\]
Consequently the balanced middle product is obtained from coefficients
$n-1,\ldots,2n-2$ of the cyclic convolution of length $L$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since $L\geq2n-1$, the product degree $3n-3$ is less than $2L$.  Reduction
modulo $x^L-1$ can therefore wrap at most once, so
\[
 [x^k]r=c_k+c_{k+L},
\]
where coefficients beyond degree $3n-3$ are zero.  If $k\geq n-1$, then
\[
 k+L\geq(n-1)+(2n-1)=3n-2>3n-3.
\]
Hence $c_{k+L}=0$ throughout the desired window.  Equivalently, every
coefficient that does wrap around lands in degrees at most $n-2$, immediately
below the middle product.
\end{proof}

Taking $L$ to be the least supported power of two gives
\[
 L=2^{\lceil\log_2(2n-1)\rceil}.
\]
This leads directly to the following transform algorithm.

\begin{algorithm}[FFT wraparound middle product]
\label{alg:fft-middle-wrap}
Input $a$ of formal length $2n-1$ and $b$ of formal length $n$.
\begin{enumerate}
 \item Let $L$ be the least supported power of two with $L\geq2n-1$.
 \item Zero-pad both inputs to length $L$ and compute their length-$L$
 Fourier transforms.
 \item Multiply the $L$ Fourier values pointwise and apply the inverse
 length-$L$ transform.
 \item Return coefficients $n-1,\ldots,2n-2$, shifted down by $n-1$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{algorithm}

\begin{theorem}[Correctness and complexity of wraparound]
\label{thm:fft-middle-wrap-complexity}
Algorithm~\ref{alg:fft-middle-wrap} returns $\operatorname{MP}_n(a,b)$.
It uses two forward transforms and one inverse transform of length $L$, together
with $L$ pointwise products.  If ring elements occupy $O(K)$ bits, root
multiplication costs $O(K)$, and a ring multiplication costs $\MZ(K)$, its bit
complexity is
\[
 O\bigl(KL\log L+L\MZ(K)\bigr).
\]
Since $2n-1\leq L<4n-2$, this is
\[
 O\bigl(Kn\log n+n\MZ(K)\bigr).
\]
The cyclic coefficients obey the same $n$-term coefficient bound as the
balanced middle product.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Correctness is Theorem~\ref{thm:fft-middle-wrap} followed by the convolution
theorem.  The operation count is three length-$L$ FFTs and $L$ pointwise
products, which gives the displayed bit bound.

For the coefficient bound, fix a residue class modulo $L$.  For each
coefficient $b_j$ there is at most one index $i$ in the support of $a$ with
$i+j$ in that residue class, because the support of $a$ has length at most
$L$.  Thus every cyclic coefficient contains at most $n$ scalar products.
The bound \eqref{eq:middle-coeff-bound} therefore remains valid, so neither
CRT reconstruction nor the $2^K+1$ specialization needs an enlarged
coefficient modulus merely because of wraparound.
\end{proof}

For comparison, computing the complete product by an ordinary padded FFT would
use the least power of two at least $3n-2$.  The wraparound length is never
larger and is sometimes half as large.  With the TFT available, however, the
comparison is less monotone: truncated ordinary convolution avoids part of a
larger transform, whereas wraparound uses a complete cyclic transform of the
smaller length.  The appropriate crossover is therefore an implementation
parameter to be measured rather than inferred only from transform lengths.

The integer-polynomial implementation provides this construction in both
exact transform backends.  The CRT-NTT version performs the cyclic convolution
modulo each word-size prime and reconstructs only the $n$ required
coefficients.  The $2^K+1$ version performs the same cyclic convolution in one
Fermat ring, using the tuned radix-$2$/MFA choice for the complete transforms.
The balanced middle-product dispatcher tunes the classical, Toom-$4/2$,
Toom-$6/3$, CRT-NTT wraparound and Fermat-ring wraparound regimes.  The two
FFT backends have separate cutoffs because the CRT cost depends on the number
of primes whereas the Fermat-ring cost depends on the coefficient size.  The
Toom-$6/3$ search begins only after the tuned classical--Toom-$4/2$ boundary,
so pairwise timing noise cannot produce an inconsistent ordering of the Toom
regimes.

\section{Low and high products in the FFT range}
\label{sec:fft-low-high-full}

At present there is no corresponding transform identity in the implementation
that computes a low or high half product with substantially less FFT work than
a complete product.  Nevertheless the FFT backends are still useful once they
beat the recursive low-product algorithms: compute the complete product with
the exact CRT-NTT or Fermat-ring multiplier and discard the unrequested half.
For a high product this direct route also avoids reversing both inputs and the
output.

\begin{algorithm}[Full-FFT edge product]
\label{alg:fft-edge-full}
Input polynomials $a,b$, a requested length $r$, and a choice
$E\in\{\mathrm{low},\mathrm{high}\}$.
\begin{enumerate}
 \item Compute the complete product $c=ab$ with the selected exact FFT backend.
 \item If $E=\mathrm{low}$, return the first $r$ coefficients of $c$.
 \item If $E=\mathrm{high}$, return the last $r$ coefficients of $c$, shifted
 down to start at degree zero.
\end{enumerate}
\end{algorithm}

\begin{theorem}
Algorithm~\ref{alg:fft-edge-full} returns the requested low or high product.
If the complete product costs $F(a,b)$ bit operations, its cost is
$F(a,b)+O(rB)$, where $B$ bounds the output coefficient size.  Thus this route
inherits the bit complexity of the complete FFT product and provides no
asymptotic short-product saving.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The requested edge is a literal coefficient window of $ab$, so correctness is
immediate.  Apart from the complete multiplication, only $r$ coefficient
copies are required.  Copying an $O(B)$-bit coefficient costs $O(B)$ bit
operations.
\end{proof}

The crossover must be timed rather than inferred from the full-product FFT
cutoff.  A recursive low product can remain faster after FFT multiplication
first becomes competitive, because it computes only triangular pieces of its
subproblems; eventually its extra recursion outweighs that saving.  The high
product has a second crossover because the reversal reduction adds linear data
movement.  The tuner therefore measures independent CRT-NTT and Fermat-ring
full-product cutoffs for low and high products, using the requested edge length
as part of the guard so a tiny edge of two large inputs never triggers a full
FFT merely because the inputs themselves are large.


\section{Taylor shift by one convolution}
\label{sec:fft-taylor-shift}

Taylor shift has a convolution form which avoids the multiplication tree of
divide-and-conquer composition.  Write
\[
 f(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1} a_i x^i,
 \qquad
 f(x+c)=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1} b_j x^j.
\]
The binomial formula gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:taylor-convolution-coeff}
 b_j=\frac1{j!}\sum_{i=j}^{n-1} a_i i!\,
          \frac{c^{i-j}}{(i-j)!}.
\end{equation}
Define two length-$n$ sequences
\begin{equation}\label{eq:taylor-convolution-sequences}
 u_{n-1-i}=a_i i!,\qquad
 v_k=\frac{c^k}{k!}.
\end{equation}
If $w=u*v$ is their ordinary convolution, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:taylor-convolution-extract}
 w_{n-1-j}=j!\,b_j.
\end{equation}
Thus one convolution computes the complete Taylor shift, provided the factorial
divisions can be carried out exactly in the transform ring.

Over $\ZZ$ one could clear all denominators in $v$ by multiplying by
$(n-1)!$, but that needlessly enlarges every transformed coefficient by about
$\log((n-1)!)$ bits.  The implementation instead works directly in a Fermat
ring
\[
 R_K=\ZZ/(2^K+1)\ZZ,
\]
with $K$ a power of two chosen both for the transform length and for signed
reconstruction of the answer.

\begin{lemma}[Factorials are units in the Fermat transform ring]
\label{lem:taylor-factorial-units}
Let $K$ be a power of two and suppose $n\leq K$.  Then every integer
$1,\ldots,n-1$, and hence every factorial $j!$ with $j<n$, is a unit modulo
$2^K+1$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $q$ be a prime divisor of $2^K+1$.  Since $2^K\equiv-1\pmod q$, the order
of $2$ modulo $q$ does not divide $K$.  It divides $2K$, and because $K$ is a
power of two the only divisor of $2K$ which does not divide $K$ is $2K$
itself.  Hence $2K\mid q-1$, so $q\geq2K+1>n$.  Therefore no prime divisor of
$2^K+1$ divides any integer from $1$ through $n-1$.
\end{proof}

For a transform length $L$ equal to the least power of two with
$L\geq2n-1$, one has $n\leq L/2$.  The SSA parameter choice enforces
$K\geq L/2$, so the hypothesis of Lemma~\ref{lem:taylor-factorial-units} is
automatic.

\begin{algorithm}[FFT Taylor shift by convolution]
\label{alg:fft-taylor-shift}
Input $f=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}a_ix^i\in\ZZ[x]$ and $c\in\ZZ$.
\begin{enumerate}
 \item If $n=0$ or $c=0$, return $f$.
 \item Let $L$ be the least power of two with $L\geq2n-1$.
 Choose a power of two $K\geq L/2$ such that every coefficient of
 $f(x+c)$ has absolute value less than $(2^K+1)/2$.
 \item In $R_K$, form
 \[
   u_{n-1-i}=a_i i!,\qquad v_k=c^k(k!)^{-1}
   \quad(0\leq i,k<n).
 \]
 \item Zero-pad to length $L$, compute two forward transforms, multiply all
 $L$ transform values pointwise, and apply the inverse transform to obtain
 $w=u*v$ in $R_K$.
 \item For $0\leq j<n$, set
 \[
   b_j=w_{n-1-j}(j!)^{-1}\in R_K,
 \]
 and return the centered integer representatives $b_j$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{algorithm}

\begin{theorem}[Correctness of convolution Taylor shift]
\label{thm:fft-taylor-shift}
Algorithm~\ref{alg:fft-taylor-shift} returns $f(x+c)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Lemma~\ref{lem:taylor-factorial-units} makes every inverse factorial in the
algorithm well defined.  The convolution theorem and
\eqref{eq:taylor-convolution-sequences} give
\[
 w_{n-1-j}
 =\sum_{i=j}^{n-1}a_i i!\frac{c^{i-j}}{(i-j)!}
 =j!\,b_j
\]
by \eqref{eq:taylor-convolution-coeff}.  Multiplication by $(j!)^{-1}$
therefore recovers $b_j$ modulo $2^K+1$.  The modulus was chosen larger than
twice the absolute value of every result coefficient, so centered
reconstruction gives the unique integer coefficient $b_j$.
\end{proof}

A convenient reconstruction bound follows from
\[
 \|f(x+c)\|_1\leq\|f\|_1(1+|c|)^{n-1}.
\]
The implementation computes the bit length of this bound without clearing the
factorial denominators: it forms $\|f\|_1$ and $(1+|c|)^{n-1}$ only for the
purpose of selecting $K$.  Thus the extra $(n-1)!$ coefficient growth of the
naive integral convolution never enters the transform.

\begin{theorem}[Complexity of convolution Taylor shift]
\label{thm:fft-taylor-shift-complexity}
Let $L$ and $K$ be as in Algorithm~\ref{alg:fft-taylor-shift}.  With
$O(K)$-bit Fermat-ring elements, root multiplications costing $O(K)$, and a
ring multiplication costing $\MZ(K)$, the bit complexity is
\[
 O\bigl(KL\log L+L\MZ(K)\bigr).
\]
Since $L<4n$ for $n>1$, this is
\[
 O\bigl(Kn\log n+n\MZ(K)\bigr).
\]
The setup and final inverse-factorial scaling require only $O(n)$ additional
ring operations.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
There are two forward length-$L$ transforms, one inverse transform and $L$
pointwise products.  The factorial, inverse-factorial and power recurrences
use only linearly many further modular operations, together with one modular
inversion.  The displayed bound follows from the FFT cost, and
$2n-1\leq L<4n-2$ gives the second form.
\end{proof}

This removes the extra multiplication-tree level present in divide-and-conquer
composition when polynomial multiplication is quasi-linear.  Its practical
crossover is nevertheless phase-sensitive because both the transform length
$L$ and the Fermat modulus size $K$ change by powers of two.  The implementation
therefore exposes the convolution method directly as
\texttt{taylor\_shift\_conv}; the existing automatic Taylor-shift dispatcher is
left unchanged until a phase-aware policy is justified by tuning rather than by
a single machine-specific cutoff.

\section{Number-theoretic transforms and CRT multiplication}

Let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ntt-prime-form}
 p=c2^s+1
\end{equation}
be an odd prime.  The multiplicative group $\mathbb F_p^\times$ is cyclic of
order $c2^s$.  Hence it contains an element $\rho$ of order $2^s$.  If
$N=2^r\leq2^s$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ntt-root}
 \omega=\rho^{2^{s-r}}
\end{equation}
has exact order $N$.  Since $\mathbb F_p$ is a field, $\omega$ is principal,
and since $p$ is odd, $N$ is invertible modulo $p$.

The form \eqref{eq:ntt-prime-form} is therefore attractive because it supplies
a large power-of-two transform length.  If $p$ fits in one machine word,
residue arithmetic and stored twiddle factors also have fixed word size.  The
constant $c$ need not be small; what matters algebraically is the large
$2$-power dividing $p-1$.

\begin{algorithm}[One-prime NTT convolution]\label{alg:one-prime-ntt}
Choose a power of two $N\geq m+n-1$ supported by $p$.
Reduce the coefficients of $f$ and $g$ modulo $p$, pad to length $N$, apply
two radix-$2$ transforms with \eqref{eq:ntt-root}, multiply pointwise, and
apply the inverse transform.  Retain the first $m+n-1$ residues.
\end{algorithm}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:one-prime-ntt-correct}
Algorithm~\ref{alg:one-prime-ntt} returns the coefficients of $fg$ modulo
$p$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Equation \eqref{eq:ntt-root} supplies a principal $N$th root and $N$ is a unit.
Theorem~\ref{thm:ring-convolution} therefore computes cyclic convolution
modulo $p$.  Corollary~\ref{cor:zero-padded-convolution} identifies this with
the ordinary product because of the zero padding.
\end{proof}

To recover an integer product, use several distinct NTT primes.  Suppose
$\bits(a_i)\leq\tau_A$, $\bits(b_j)\leq\tau_B$, and put
$s_0=\min(m,n)$.  As in \eqref{eq:product-coefficient-bound}, every product
coefficient $d_k$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ntt-coefficient-bound}
 |d_k|<s_0 2^{\tau_A+\tau_B}.
\end{equation}
Let $p_1,\ldots,p_t$ be distinct supported NTT primes and put
$M=p_1\cdots p_t$.

\begin{theorem}[Exact CRT reconstruction]\label{thm:ntt-crt-correct}
If
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ntt-crt-size}
 M\geq
 2^{\tau_A+\tau_B+\lceil\log_2s_0\rceil+1},
\end{equation}
then the balanced representative in $(-M/2,M/2)$ of the CRT combination of
the NTT residues is exactly each integer coefficient of $fg$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{eq:ntt-coefficient-bound},
\[
 2|d_k|<2^{\tau_A+\tau_B+\lceil\log_2s_0\rceil+1}\leq M.
\]
The one-prime transforms determine $d_k$ modulo every $p_i$, hence modulo
$M$ by the Chinese remainder theorem.  Since $|d_k|<M/2$, it is the unique
integer in the balanced interval representing that residue class.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Complexity of CRT-NTT multiplication]
Let $N$ be the least power of two at least $m+n-1$.  Suppose the available
NTT primes have $w=\Theta(1)$ machine-word bits and sufficient transform
length.  Put
\[
 W=\tau_A+\tau_B+\lceil\log_2s_0\rceil+1.
\]
Then $t=O(W/w)$ primes suffice.  The transforms and pointwise products use
\[
 O(tN\log N)
\]
word modular operations.  With product-tree CRT, reconstruction has bit
complexity
\[
 O\bigl(N\MZ(W)\log t\bigr).
\]
A straightforward incremental CRT has the same correctness but may have a
larger reconstruction constant and an additional factor up to $t$ in a simple
worst-case bit bound.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
A product of $t$ $w$-bit primes has $\Theta(tw)$ bits, so
\eqref{eq:ntt-crt-size} needs $t=O(W/w)$.  By
Theorem~\ref{thm:fft-operation-count}, two forward transforms and one inverse
