transform cost $O(N\log N)$ word operations per prime; the $N$ pointwise
products do not change the order.  Standard product-tree CRT combines $t$
moduli of total bit size $W$ in $O(\MZ(W)\log t)$ bit operations per output
coefficient, giving the stated reconstruction bound.
\end{proof}

Thus an NTT trades one large transform modulus for several word-size moduli.
The transforms are cheap and regular; the price is the number of primes and
the final CRT reconstruction.

\section{Transforms modulo $2^K+1$}

A different exact strategy avoids CRT.  Let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fermat-modulus}
 P=2^K+1,
\end{equation}
where $K$ is a power of two, and work in
$R=\ZZ/P\ZZ$.  The modulus need not be prime.

\begin{lemma}[Order of two]\label{lem:fermat-two-order}
The residue class of $2$ modulo $P$ has exact multiplicative order $2K$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The relation $2^K\equiv-1\pmod P$ gives $2^{2K}\equiv1$, so the order divides
$2K$.  It does not divide $K$.  Since $K$ and $2K$ are powers of two, every
proper divisor of $2K$ divides $K$.  Therefore the order is $2K$.
\end{proof}

Let $N$ be a power of two with $N\leq2K$.  Then $N\mid2K$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ssa-root}
 \omega=2^{2K/N}\pmod P
\end{equation}
has exact order $N$.  Over a composite ring we must still prove the stronger
principal-root property.

\begin{lemma}[Principal Fermat-ring roots]\label{lem:fermat-principal-root}
The element $\omega$ in \eqref{eq:ssa-root} is a principal $N$th root of
unity in $R$.  Moreover $N$ is a unit in $R$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The modulus $P$ is odd, so the power of two $N$ is a unit.  Fix
$1\leq j<N$.  We prove that $\omega^j-1$ is a unit modulo $P$.

Suppose a prime $q$ divided both $P$ and the integer representing
$\omega^j-1$.  Since $q\mid P$, we have $2^K\equiv-1\pmod q$.  The order of
$2$ modulo $q$ therefore divides $2K$ but not $K$.  As $2K$ is a power of
two, this order must equal $2K$.  On the other hand,
$\omega^j\equiv1\pmod q$ would make that order divide
\[
 \frac{2Kj}{N}<2K,
\]
a contradiction.  Hence $\gcd(\omega^j-1,P)=1$, so $\omega^j-1$ is a unit.
The geometric identity
\[
 (\omega^j-1)\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\omega^{ij}=\omega^{jN}-1=0
\]
then implies the required orthogonality sum is zero.
\end{proof}

The same relation that supplies the roots also makes their multiplication
cheap.  Since $2^K\equiv-1$, multiplication by $2^e$ can be performed by a
bit shift followed by folding the part above bit $K$ back with a minus sign.
After reducing $e$ modulo $2K$, crossing $K$ merely changes the sign.  Thus a
twiddle multiplication costs $O(K)$ bit operations rather than a general
$K$-bit modular multiplication.

\begin{algorithm}[Single-modulus transform multiplication]\label{alg:ssa-poly-mul}
Let $f,g\in\ZZ[x]$ have lengths $m,n$.  Put $L=m+n-1$ and choose a power of
two $N\geq L$.  Choose a power of two $K$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ssa-parameter-conditions}
 N\leq2K,
 \qquad
 K\geq\tau_A+\tau_B+\lceil\log_2s_0\rceil+1.
\end{equation}
Reduce the coefficients modulo $P=2^K+1$, pad to length $N$, transform with
\eqref{eq:ssa-root}, multiply pointwise, and apply the inverse transform.
Return the balanced integer representatives of the first $L$ residues.
\end{algorithm}

\begin{theorem}[Correctness of single-modulus multiplication]
Algorithm~\ref{alg:ssa-poly-mul} returns the exact product $fg$ in $\ZZ[x]$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The first inequality in \eqref{eq:ssa-parameter-conditions} ensures that the
principal $N$th root of Lemma~\ref{lem:fermat-principal-root} exists.  Hence
the transform computes the coefficient convolution modulo $P$ by
Theorem~\ref{thm:ring-convolution} and
Corollary~\ref{cor:zero-padded-convolution}.

The second inequality and \eqref{eq:ntt-coefficient-bound} give
\[
 2|d_k|<2^K<P.
\]
Thus $|d_k|<P/2$, so the true integer coefficient is the unique balanced
representative of its residue modulo $P$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Bit complexity]\label{thm:ssa-poly-complexity}
With $N,K$ as in Algorithm~\ref{alg:ssa-poly-mul}, suppose multiplication of
two $K$-bit integers costs $\MZ(K)$.  Then the transform multiplication has
bit complexity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ssa-complexity}
 O\bigl(NK\log N+N\MZ(K)\bigr),
\end{equation}
and uses $O(NK)$ bits of transform storage.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
There are $O(N\log N)$ butterflies in the two forward and one inverse
transforms.  Additions, subtractions and multiplication by the power-of-two
twiddles each cost $O(K)$ bits, so all transform stages together cost
$O(NK\log N)$.  Normalization by $N^{-1}$ is also multiplication by a power
of two modulo $P$ and costs $O(NK)$.

There are $N$ pointwise products of $K$-bit residues, costing
$O(N\MZ(K))$.  Encoding, balanced reconstruction and zero padding touch
$O(NK)$ bits and are absorbed.  The transform arrays contain $O(N)$ residues
of $O(K)$ bits each.
\end{proof}

The constraint $N\leq2K$ is important.  This simple one-modulus method is most
naturally aimed at the regime in which coefficient size is at least comparable
to polynomial length.  More elaborate Sch\"onhage--Strassen constructions
split data recursively and do not retain this restriction in the same simple
form.

\section{NTT versus $2^K+1$ transforms}

Both methods use exactly the same Fourier and convolution theorems.  Their
arithmetic costs are distributed differently.

For an NTT, every transform is over a word-size prime, so butterfly arithmetic
is inexpensive; large output coefficients require several transforms and CRT
reconstruction.  For a $2^K+1$ transform, one large residue ring suffices and
CRT disappears.  The twiddle factors are powers of two, so they reduce to
shifts and signed folds, but the $N$ pointwise products are genuine $K$-bit
multiplications.

This distinction explains the useful parameter regimes.  NTT multiplication
is attractive when enough suitable word primes are available and the number of
CRT primes is moderate.  The $2^K+1$ method becomes attractive when coefficient
size is large relative to length, because it avoids repeating the entire
transform for many CRT primes while retaining cheap twiddle multiplication.

Cache-aware matrix Fourier algorithms change the order in which the same
butterflies are performed.  Truncated Fourier transforms avoid work associated
with known zero inputs or unwanted outputs.  Neither changes the algebraic
correctness arguments of this chapter, so they can be added later without
changing the transform interface or the convolution proofs.
